## intake-2386#record — Locals (Generated-Computers/Locals): agent-oriented local inference server for Apple silicon (MLX), small-batch kernels, GPU grammar masks, recurrent-state prompt cache, multi-agent harness

- **URL**: https://github.com/Generated-Computers/Locals
- **Verification**: dive-verified

- **Disposition**: integrated

### Claims (as verified)

0. README @0d39e161 (retrieved 2026-10-22), self-reported: with 8 concurrent requests on an M2 Pro 16 GB and Qwen3.5-4B 4-bit (affine, group 64), Locals decodes 271 tok/s vs 87 for mlx-lm (3.1x), measured in process with fixed 256-token replies (EOS ignored); with one request about equal (64 vs 61). A client-counted run through each server's own API gives 244 vs 75 (3.24x) at 8 requests and 65 vs 51 (1.27x) at 1 (docs/benchmarks section 1). No independent replication found.
   - *narrowed*: Numbers confirmed verbatim in README.md:L122 and L177-178 and docs/measurements:L22-28 (in-process, 256-token replies, EOS ignored, median of two rounds). Added the client-counted server run (244 vs 75, 3.24x; 65 vs 51 at 1). Self-reported by the authors; the M2 Max and M5 Pro runs are the same authors or a user report relayed by them; no independent replication found by this dive.

1. docs/why-faster @0d39e161, self-reported one-at-a-time ablation (M2 Pro, Qwen3.5-4B, 8 requests): Locals 272.7 tok/s; Locals' model under mlx-lm's BatchGenerator 272.9 (the gain is model computation, not scheduling); without the small-batch kernels 119.2; bf16 instead of fp16 97.9; without fused projections 246.8 (4-10% at 4-16 requests); without the GDN kernel 267.6 (1-2%); without CPU/GPU pipelining 236.5 (13-16% as a reduction, 15-19% as a speed-up). The bf16 row conflates bf16 emulation with a tuning-cache fallback: for bf16 keys the shipped preset holds the fp16-only fragh kernel on 5 of the 6 Qwen3.5-4B matmul shapes at 4 and 8 rows, and kernels._choose maps that to MLX's baseline rather than the stored next-best frag; at 16 rows (no fragh) bf16 costs only 8% (317.0 vs 345.8). On an M5 Pro fused projections are worth 33% at 8 requests and fp16 gives nothing (docs/benchmarks section 2).
   - *narrowed*: All six ablation numbers confirmed (docs/why-faster:L31-41). Reattributed in part: the bf16 collapse at 4-8 rows coincides with the shipped g14s preset holding fragh for 5/6 Qwen3.5-4B bf16 shapes at M=4 and M=8 (0/6 at M=16), and kernels.py:L561-562 maps a bf16 fragh choice to ('mlx',) although the '|frag' family winner is stored next to it; so the row measures 'fp16 + a fallback artifact', not bf16 emulation alone. Inference from source + preset, not a re-measurement (no compute). Fusion gain is hardware-dependent (33% on M5 Pro, docs/benchmarks:L141-146).

2. kernels.py and mma_kernel.py @0d39e161, checked against MLX v0.32.0 quantized.cpp: on M1/M2 MLX's affine 4-bit qmv runs one input row per threadgroup (its weight-reusing qmv_wide is gen-15+ only) and switches to a tiled split-K qmm at M >= 6 for large matrices (10 or 14 for smaller ones), a 32-row tile per Locals' note. Locals routes 4-bit affine group-64 matmuls with 1 <= M <= 32 rows to the fastest of mlx, multi, v3, small, frag, fragh and mma (nax on M5), timed on the device per (N, K, M, dtype) after a correctness filter (max abs error <= 0.05*(1+max|ref|) vs MLX on a fixed-key random input), with a 10% margin over MLX, cached per GPU architecture with shipped presets, background idle tuning up to 16 rows and an optional whole-step pipelined family override. At M = 1 every shipped choice is MLX's kernel.
   - *narrowed*: Confirmed in Locals source and in MLX v0.32.0 quantized.cpp (get_qmv_batch_limit: 6/10/14 on gen 13/14 non-'d' parts; qmv_wide only gen >= 15 for affine). The candidate set is wider than the Stage-1 text (v3, small, nax). The chosen kernel passes a per-shape correctness filter at 5%-of-max-abs tolerance against MLX; no model-level check of the chosen kernels at load (tests only). At M = 1 all 79 shipped choices are MLX.

3. README/docs @0d39e161, NARROWED: tool calls and JSON decode under a grammar whose state stays on the GPU. There are two paths. The default token-table path (LocalsController) keeps a (Q, V) allowed mask and a transition table on the device. The --walk path (WalkController) uses no table: a Metal kernel walks every token through the byte DFA at each step. Forced literals are fed to the model without being sampled; they are NOT skipped by the model. In the single-stream engine a forced span rides in the same forward pass as the preceding sampled token. In the server's default batch engine (drip mode) a span is fed one token per step. The guarantee that a reply cut by max_tokens still closes its call holds where budget-aware masking runs: the table path (Engine and LocalsController rows in the batch engine). The walk path and the mlx-lm logits_processor have no budget mask, and --typed-tools is meant to run with --walk.
   - *narrowed*: "GPU-resident grammar transition table": true on the default table path; the --walk path keeps no table. "forced tokens written without running the model": they are fed through the model unsampled (in the same pass as the preceding sampled token in Engine, or one per step in the server's drip batch mode); no forward pass is skipped. "truncated reply still closes its call": holds only where the budget mask runs (table path); WalkController inherits no-op budget hooks (engine.py:L157-L162; walk.py:L124 class has no budget override), and server.py:L2219-L2220 shows --walk is opt-in and --typed-tools is paired with it. tests/test_forced.py and tests/test_formats.py carry no truncation test; tests/test_pda.py:L232 tests budget masking on the pushdown path only. A server under --walk --typed-tools therefore has no truncation-closure guarantee per its own docs.

Text that the grammar forces is fed to the model without being sampled."

4. docs/why-faster + docs/prompt-cache + docs/measurements @0d39e161 and commit 76f5502 (2026-10-12): for models whose cache cannot be cut back to a prefix (Gated DeltaNet recurrent layers, sliding windows; detected as any layer not a plain KVCache) a cached entry is reusable only at exact stored points, so Server._checkpoints places snapshots at positions derived by rendering the conversation with the model's own template: the system part (end, or the start of the session-specific block), the start of the last message, and the reply start as the NEXT request will render it. The 83% vs 28% prompt-tokens-from-cache figure (four concurrent synthetic multi-turn conversations for four minutes; machine/model only by the measurements-doc default M2 Pro + Qwen3.5-4B, model not named in the commit) is an ablation of the REPLY checkpoint alone (table header 'Reply checkpoint off | On'; system and user checkpoints present in both arms); same run 36 vs 23 turns, median TTFT 5.2 vs 10.6 s. The replay script that produced it is not in the repo.

10. server.py _guard/_looping/_line_looping/_run @0d39e161: reply guards cut a reply on max_calls (6), duplicate identical calls, a token-period loop (block <=256 tokens repeated 3x over >=24 tokens; blocks <8 tokens must span 128), or 30 sentence-length lines with <=6 distinct (tools offered, before a call opens); thresholds rise inside call arguments (16 repeats over 256 tokens plus 1,024 grace tokens) and without tools (12 repeats over 96 plus grace). A reply repeating the previous turn's identical call is re-decoded with a model-voiced steering prefix quoting the earlier result (max_tokens capped at 2,048), then up to twice more with the repeated tools banned, then ends the turn with an 'I am stuck' message unless tool_choice forces a call.

14. qmv_frag.py @0d39e161: frag/fragh compute Y^T = W X^T with 8x8 simdgroup MMA so the activation rows are the 8-wide side (MT = ceil(M/8); one compiled kernel serves 8 row counts), dequantizing 4-bit weights straight into register A-fragments with no threadgroup memory and no barriers; K is permuted so each lane reads its 32 weights of a 128-wide chunk as one uint4 and every weight is read once. fragh uses half fragments (nibble | 0x6400 exponent trick, one half2 fma for scale and bias) and is fp16-only. In the shipped M2 preset frag/fragh win most shapes from 5 to 24 rows; multi wins at 2, v3 at 3.

## intake-2388#record — Breaking the Space Barrier and its Application to Language Model Inference

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09139
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty high, relevance high; categories: tool_implementation, inference_serving, hardware_optimization)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2386#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Abstract (v1, 2026-10-06): the per-state token step graph (vocabulary trie x deterministic byte automaton) is a mangrove (one path per token); total few-path reachability (STCON_sf) is placed in ReachUL, LOGDCFL, C=L and SC2 with O(log^2 n / log log n) space. Theory stated, proofs (App. B) NOT read; out of EPYC scope.
   - *unaffected*: Theory claim restated from the abstract and Sec. 3; proofs (App. B, C) not read and not checked. No EPYC decision depends on it.
   - anchor `Abstract, arXiv 2610.09139v1 HTML` @ arXiv 2610.09139v1 HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22T09:18Z, sha256 026328fdaabe: "one can decide whether such a graph connects two points while verifying that it really has few paths, with very little memory"
1. Sec. 4 / App. D: six engine mechanisms: (1) table-free mask, a Metal kernel with one thread per (token, row) that walks the token bytes through a 256x|Q| int16 automaton each step (7-18 us per step on M2 Pro, author-measured); (2) forced chains fed in the same model run as the preceding sampled token; (3) forced text committed in the tokenizer's own (canonical) split; (4) budget-aware masking from a per-state shortest-completion table; (5) recursive schemas via a capped (64) GPU stack; (6) certified block-parallel decoding of independent object fields, verified token-identical to sequential greedy.
   - *narrowed*: Mechanisms confirmed from Sec. 4 + App. D. Precision: forced text is fed THROUGH the model (it builds KV and recurrent state); what it saves is separate decode passes, not model compute. In the batch engine (App. D.7 drip mode) each forced token takes one row-step, so forced spans save sampling/masking but not steps for that row. The walk kernel keeps a sparse-head CPU path for states with <=4096 allowed tokens (about 4 ms on the CPU per state, computed once).
   - anchor `Sec. 4 item 2` @ arXiv 2610.09139v1 HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22T09:18Z, sha256 026328fdaabe: "After each sampled token the engine feeds the whole forced continuation in the same model run, so it costs nothing extra"
   - anchor `Sec. 4 item 1` @ arXiv 2610.09139v1 HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22T09:18Z, sha256 026328fdaabe: "a GPU kernel runs one thread per token, walks the token’s bytes through the automaton and writes the state it reaches"
2. Abstract/Table 1, NARROWED: vs MLX+llguidance on one M2 Pro, Qwen3.5-2B/4B 4-bit, greedy: six JSON extractions 1.28x (2B) / 1.18x (4B) faster than llguidance fast-forward in the same decode loop and kernels; "3 MB instead of up to 1.5 GB" is vs a precomputed token TABLE (Outlines-style), not vs llguidance (llguidance <0.5 MB for 2 of 3 schemas, fails to compile the 1,106-state typed tool grammar); "sixteen tool-calling agents 2.5x" is a whole-engine comparison vs stock mlx-lm that the paper itself says includes its matrix kernels and batching (unconstrained 8-stream decode is also 3.06x vs stock), and at 8 agents the engine is 0.87x (slower) in wall time.
   - *narrowed*: 1.2-1.3x is real but single-request, greedy, same-kernel; "3 MB vs 1.5 GB" is vs token tables, not llguidance (Table 9: llguidance <0.5 MB person / 96-field; 4.7 MB for sixteen grammars vs stcon 4.9 MB; llguidance fails only the typed tool grammar); the 2.5x agent number is confounded by the authors' own batched matrix kernels (unconstrained decode is 3.06x vs stock at 8 streams, Table 21) and reverses to 0.87x wall at 8 agents; checks passed 10/16 (stcon) vs 11/16 (stock).
   - anchor `Sec. 5.6 Table 13 caption` @ arXiv 2610.09139v1 HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22T09:18Z, sha256 026328fdaabe: "These numbers also include our matrix kernels and batching, which are engineering rather than theory."
   - anchor `Sec. 5.4` @ arXiv 2610.09139v1 HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22T09:18Z, sha256 026328fdaabe: "A precomputed mask table grows with the grammar and stays in memory for the life of the server: 77 MB for a five-field schema, 824 MB for a tool-call grammar, 1.5 GB for a 96-field form."
   - anchor `App. E.6, Table 22 discussion` @ arXiv 2610.09139v1 HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22T09:18Z, sha256 026328fdaabe: "At eight agents our wall time is longer although the throughput is"
   - anchor `App. E.5, Table 21 discussion` @ arXiv 2610.09139v1 HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22T09:18Z, sha256 026328fdaabe: "mlx-lm’s own loop on our patched model reaches our numbers, so the gain is in the kernels and fp16, not the loop"
3. Sec. 5.1 Table 5 (appended): the single-request speedup decomposes into fewer runs (226 vs 343 runs for six outputs; 0.69 runs/token vs 1.00 mask-only, 0.72 llguidance fast-forward; 31% of tokens forced) times cheaper runs (8.3 vs 9.7 ms, because the mask never waits on the host).
   - *unaffected*: Appended: the clean, like-for-like result of the paper.
   - anchor `Sec. 5.1` @ arXiv 2610.09139v1 HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22T09:18Z, sha256 026328fdaabe: "so the six outputs need 226 runs instead of 343 (Table 5)"
4. Sec. 7 / App. E.5 (appended): the forced-span gain shrinks as batching makes a step compute-bound and disappears at 16 slots on the M2 Pro; memory gains remain.
   - *unaffected*: Appended: the paper's own limitation; it decides portability to batched serving.
   - anchor `Sec. 7 Discussion` @ arXiv 2610.09139v1 HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22T09:18Z, sha256 026328fdaabe: "When a server batches many requests so that each step is limited by arithmetic rather than memory, extra tokens in a run stop being free, and the first gain shrinks (in our batch at 16 slots it disappears), while the memory gains remain."
   - anchor `App. E.5 Fig. 11 caption` @ arXiv 2610.09139v1 HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22T09:18Z, sha256 026328fdaabe: "level at 16, where a step is bound by arithmetic and forced spans buy nothing on a uniform batch"
5. App. D.8 / E.3 (appended): in the Qwen XML tool-call reply grammar every tag is a forced span, 20-30% of a call's tokens are fed not sampled (30% typed, 20% untyped); a reply cut by the token budget still closes its call.
   - *unaffected*: Appended: the only per-call forced-fraction number for tool calls; measured on the authors' agent suite, Qwen XML format.
   - anchor `App. D.8` @ arXiv 2610.09139v1 HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22T09:18Z, sha256 026328fdaabe: "Every tag of a call is a forced span (20–30% of a call’s tokens are fed, not sampled), and a reply cut by the token budget still closes its call."
6. Sec. 5.5 / App. G (appended): budget-aware masking makes 90/90 JSON outputs valid at every feasible token limit vs 3/90 without; recursive 54/61 vs 2/61 (not all).
   - *unaffected*: Appended. Code check (Locals docs @0d39e161): budget masking is NOT active on the walk-kernel path; see intake-2386#record claim 3 update.
   - anchor `Sec. 5.5` @ arXiv 2610.09139v1 HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22T09:18Z, sha256 026328fdaabe: "every JSON output is valid under every limit that admits a valid output at all, 90 of 90, against 3 of 90 without it, and for recursive schemas 54 of 61 against 2 of 61"
7. Sec. 5.5 / App. I (appended): certified parallel fields cut model runs 1.80x (2B) / 1.42x (4B) with token-identical output but give NO wall-time gain on the M2 Pro; speculative drafts in the batch engine and prompt-lookup drafts under the constraint were measured as near-zero gains (2-token step at 8 rows costs 1.48x; lookup 1.02x overall).
   - *unaffected*: Appended negative results: parallel fields save runs, not wall time; speculative drafts under constraint ~1.02x.
   - anchor `Sec. 5.5` @ arXiv 2610.09139v1 HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22T09:18Z, sha256 026328fdaabe: "On this laptop GPU extra rows in a batch are not free, so the wall time does not drop yet"
   - anchor `App. I Table 34` @ arXiv 2610.09139v1 HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22T09:18Z, sha256 026328fdaabe: "batched steps are compute-bound"

## intake-2391#record — Pre$^3$: Enabling Deterministic Pushdown Automata for Faster Structured LLM Generation

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03887
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: tool_implementation, inference_serving)
- **Disposition**: knowledge_only
- **Expanded from**: intake-2388#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Abstract (v1), NARROWED: Pre3 turns an LR(1) transition graph into a DPDA by precomputing prefix-conditioned edges (accepted symbol + stack-match condition + stack ops). This removes context-dependent tokens and runtime path exploration, and lets stack-match conditions be checked in parallel. The abstract reports TPOT up to -40% and throughput up to +36%. In the body the -40% is one cell (DeepSeek-V2-Lite, batch 128, Table 2: 40.78%). Most other cells are 1-30%, and Qwen2-14B INT8 is 0.1-18.7%. The +36% throughput figure is NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE in the body text: Sec. 4.4 says "up to 20% higher throughput" and the conclusion says "up to 40% in throughput".
   - *narrowed*: Mechanism confirmed (Sec. 3.1-3.3). -40% TPOT is a single best cell. The +36% throughput figure does not appear in the body; Sec. 4.4 says 20% and the conclusion says 40%, so the paper is internally inconsistent. The paper does not compare against the Stage-1 reference intake-2388#record (it predates it).
   - anchor `Abstract` @ arXiv 2506.03887v1 HTML retrieved 2026-10-22T09:18Z sha256 efccb77c8dd4: "reducing time per output token (TPOT) by up to 40% and increasing throughput by up to 36% in our experiments"
   - anchor `Sec. 4.4` @ arXiv 2506.03887v1 HTML: "achieving up to 20% higher throughput at higher concurrency levels"
   - anchor `Sec. 6 Conclusion` @ arXiv 2506.03887v1 HTML: "Pre3 significantly outperforms existing SOTA baselines by up to 40% in throughput"
1. Sec. 4 (appended): implementation is ~2,000 lines Python + ~1,000 C++ inside LightLLM. Hardware is Xeon 6448Y + H800 GPUs. Baselines are XGrammar, Outlines (vLLM) and llama.cpp. The per-step overhead figure claims superiority over llama.cpp (Fig. 6, image; values not in text). Datasets are NousResearch json-mode-eval plus unpublished private data.
   - *unaffected*: Appended. The llama.cpp comparison is a figure without values in text and uses an unstated llama.cpp version; not usable as a number about our frozen tree.
   - anchor `Sec. 4.1` @ arXiv 2506.03887v1 HTML: "We implemented our approach in 2,000 lines of Python code and about 1,000 lines of C++ code, and we seamlessly integrated with LightLLM"
   - anchor `Sec. 4.2` @ arXiv 2506.03887v1 HTML: "Pre3 demonstrates a superiority over Outlines and llama.cpp, and Pre3 remains a consistent advantage over XGrammar."
2. Sec. 2.1 Table 1 (appended): XGrammar adds up to 60% per-token latency at batch 512 on Llama-3-8B / 2xH800 (147.64 vs 92.23 ms). The text calls this "up to 37.5% higher latency", which is the same pair of numbers expressed relative to the larger value. Grammar overhead grows with batch size because validation is not parallelized.
   - *unaffected*: Appended. Batch-512 GPU regime; EPYC serving runs np=1..8, so the batch-scaling argument mostly does not transfer.

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-22 Stage-2 dive: read abstract, Sec. 1-2.3, 3 intro + 3.1, 4.1-4.4, 5, 6 of v1 HTML. The +36% throughput figure is NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE in the body (20% in Sec. 4.4, 40% in the conclusion). -40% TPOT is one table cell. The llama.cpp baseline appears only in a figure.

## intake-2393#record — ml-explore/mlx issue 3661: ~3x bandwidth drop from GEMV (M=1) to tiled GEMM for skinny M (M=2-64), the continuous-batching decode regime

- **URL**: https://github.com/ml-explore/mlx/issues/3661
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: hardware_optimization, quantization, inference_serving)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2386#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Issue body (opened 2026-06-11 by hvasconcelos, closed by its author 22 minutes later with no comments or maintainer reply; footer says it was generated with Claude Code): on an M1 Pro (mlx 0.31.2), 4-bit quantized_matmul on a 151936x1024 weight costs 0.703/1.195/2.166/3.384 ms at M = 1/2/4/8 (sub-linear by 8 rows: 4.8x) and then stays flat to M = 32 (3.134 ms) before doubling at 64; fp16 x@W.T costs about 5.6 ms (56 GB/s) for every M from 2 to 64 against 1.933 ms (161 GB/s) at M = 1.
   - *narrowed*: Numbers confirmed verbatim. 'Near-linear' holds to M = 4 (3.1x) but not to 8 (4.8x for 8x the rows), then flat to 32, which matches MLX v0.32.0's per-row qmv up to get_qmv_batch_limit then a tiled qmm. Third-party and independent of Locals, but single-author, AI-assisted, self-closed, with no maintainer confirmation.
   - anchor `issue body (api.github.com, retrieved 2026-10-22)` @ api.github.com/repos/ml-explore/mlx/issues/3661 retrieved 2026-10-22 (issue updated_at 2026-06-11T19:46:42Z): "- **4-bit `quantized_matmul`**: cost grows near-linearly in M at small M (0.70 → 1.20 → 2.17 → 3.38 ms for M=1/2/4/8), i.e. the weight sweep is effectively re-paid per row."
   - anchor `issue body (api.github.com, retrieved 2026-10-22)` @ api.github.com/repos/ml-explore/mlx/issues/3661 retrieved 2026-10-22 (issue updated_at 2026-06-11T19:46:42Z): "| 8 | 5.598 ms | 56 | 3.384 ms |"
1. Issue body: on the same M1 Pro, llama.cpp b8470 (same GGUF in f16, Qwen3-0.6B) keeps per-request decode at 83.0/82.4/78.1/46.4 tok/s for B = 1/2/4/8 against 101.3/39.0/37.9/36.1 for the author's MLX engine; the author attributes this to llama.cpp's Metal mul_mv kernels amortizing one weight sweep over several rows.
   - *unaffected*: Appended: the issue's own llama.cpp baseline. Contrast: Locals' docs/measurements L232-235 show llama.cpp b11724 Metal on hybrid Qwen3.5-4B Q4_K_M staying at 44/43/46 tok/s for 1/2/4 requests, so llama.cpp's amortization is model/quant dependent on Metal.
   - anchor `issue body (api.github.com, retrieved 2026-10-22)` @ api.github.com/repos/ml-explore/mlx/issues/3661 retrieved 2026-10-22 (issue updated_at 2026-06-11T19:46:42Z): "| llama.cpp b8470, same GGUF f16, same hardware | 83.0 | **82.4** | **78.1** | **46.4** |"
2. libmlxforge PR #39 (merged 2026-06-12, author self-report): multi-row GEMV Metal kernels for dense fp16 with B in [2,16] read each weight row once for all B activation rows (fp32 register accumulators, simd_sum) and reach about 161 GB/s, full GEMV bandwidth, at B <= 4 and 125 GB/s at B = 8 (two output columns per simdgroup); quantized weights are not covered.
   - *unaffected*: Appended from the author's cross-referenced downstream PR (api.github.com, retrieved 2026-10-22); an independent implementation of the multi-row GEMV mechanism for dense fp16; author self-report, not reproduced.

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-22 Stage-2 dive (kernels): claim 0 numbers confirmed, 'near-linear' narrowed to M <= 4; recorded that the author closed the issue unanswered and fixed it downstream (libmlxforge PR #39, multi-row GEMV for dense fp16, ~161 GB/s at B <= 4); appended the issue's llama.cpp per-row baseline and the downstream result; MLX v0.32.0 later added qmv_wide (gen-15+ only for affine) and split-K qmm for small M.

## intake-2394#record — Combining Guided Decoding and Speculative Decoding: Making CPU and GPU Cooperate Seamlessly (TensorRT-LLM tech blog 12, NVIDIA + XGrammar teams)

- **URL**: https://nvidia.github.io/TensorRT-LLM/1.2.1/blogs/tech_blog/blog12_Combining_Guided_Decoding_and_Speculative_Decoding.html
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: speculative_decoding, tool_implementation, inference_serving)
- **Disposition**: knowledge_only
- **Expanded from**: intake-2388#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Body read 2026-10-22 (blog12, TRT-LLM 1.2.1 docs, sha256 d952488a7242): with speculative decoding, guided decoding must advance the grammar state and fill a mask for every token in the target verification batch (new token + drafts), then roll the state back by the number of rejected tokens. XGrammar and LLGuidance support this rollback natively. Masking only the target keeps outputs correct, because draft tokens that violate the grammar get rejected, but it lowers acceptance.
   - *unaffected*: Stage-1 only had the table of contents; body now read. Confirms rollback-on-reject as the standard pattern.
   - anchor `Trace Grammar State > Target Model` @ nvidia.github.io/TensorRT-LLM/1.2.1 blog12, fetched 2026-10-22, sha256 d952488a7242: "After verification, the grammar state should be rolled back by the number of rejected tokens."
   - anchor `Trace Grammar State > Target Model` @ blog12 sha256 d952488a7242: "any draft tokens violating the grammar will be forcefully rejected by the verification step. However, this hurts the acceptance rate."
1. Body: for logit-sampling drafters (draft-target, EAGLE, MTP) the grammar is also applied to each draft step and rolled back after drafting. Masks are reused when draft and target share a vocabulary. EAGLE3 pruned-vocab drafters need a d2t id map fused into the mask kernel. Tree drafting would need per-branch advance/rollback and is not covered.
   - *unaffected*: New from body.
2. Body: the grammar backends run on CPU. Mask generation is overlapped with the GPU forward. For one-model speculative decoding inside one CUDA graph, CPU grammar work is made capturable with cudaLaunchHostFunc callbacks on a dedicated stream, synchronized by token/mask events. Engineering hazards are documented: host/callback data races, and a GIL vs CUDA-mutex deadlock.
   - *unaffected*: Stage-1 "CUDA-graph-capturable grammar callbacks" confirmed. The mechanism is CUDA-specific (cudaLaunchHostFunc); HIP has hipLaunchHostFunc, and the port is judged in the report.
   - anchor `Guided Decoding` @ blog12 sha256 d952488a7242: "Currently, these grammar backends are implemented on CPU, so the grammar computation introduces significant CPU overhead."
   - anchor `CUDA Callback` @ blog12 sha256 d952488a7242: "Hence, we can launch grammar computation along with other auxiliary host functions as CUDA callbacks to a CUDA stream."
3. Table 1 (vendor-reported, appended): average acceptance length per iteration with draft length 3, H200, XGrammar backend. JSON Mode Eval: EAGLE3 2.86 vs 2.65 without a draft-side grammar on 8B, 2.72 vs 2.60 on 70B. JSON Schema Bench: 2.55 vs 2.33 on 8B, 2.50 vs 2.30 on 70B. NGram: 2.59 / 2.44 / 1.89 / 1.87. A draft-side grammar raises acceptance "by a slighter margin than expected" and adds mask-kernel and exposed-CPU overhead. Speculative decoding speedup with guided decoding is up to ~2x at batch size 1 (Pareto figures; values in images).
   - *unaffected*: Appended. Vendor-reported (NVIDIA + XGrammar teams), restatement-class, single hardware. Effect size of a draft-side grammar is +0.12..+0.22 acceptance length.
   - anchor `Performance and Analysis, Table 1 discussion` @ blog12 sha256 d952488a7242: "As presented, this does decrease acceptance rates, but by a slighter margin than expected."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-22 Stage-2 dive: body read in full. Added the target-side rollback rule, the draft-side masking with the EAGLE3 d2t map, the CPU-callback capture design, and Table 1 acceptance lengths (draft-side grammar +0.12..+0.22 accepted tokens per iteration at draft length 3). | 2026-10-22 Stage-2 close-out (operator pre-approved all recommended sources): dive-surfaced sources NOT dived, with reasons: vLLM PR 14702 and SGLang PR 6499 declined (draft-masking designs already covered by the blog body).

## intake-2395#record — Measure Before You Manage: Evaluating Agent Working Memory in Coding Agents

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.31057
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: memory_augmented, context_management, agent_architecture, benchmark_methodology)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2387#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Scope (Sec. 1, Relation to existing work): the paper studies TASK-LOCAL working memory inside one coding trajectory (typed objects: instructions, artifacts, tool outputs, agent state; policies: FIFO/LRU/uniform compression/object-aware compression/retrieval). It is NOT a study of cross-session lesson reuse, so it does not answer whether distilled lessons help; the Stage-1 framing that this source tests lesson reuse is wrong. Its transferable content is evaluation method.
   - *reattributed*: Stage-1 (intake-2387#record context) framed this source as evidence on reusing distilled lessons; the source is intra-trajectory working-memory management. It supplies method, not an effect size for lesson reuse.
   - anchor `Sec. 1 "Relation to existing work"` @ arXiv:2608.31057v1 (HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 eb9a7f58ef69): "Our focus is narrower: task-local working memory in coding agents and how its semantic heterogeneity should be measured, managed, and evaluated."
1. Typed-object accounting over 55 SWE trajectories (1,350 in-context objects): tool outputs are 55.5% of pooled content volume but 40.2% of retention-weighted cost; artifacts 28.3% and 38.9% (Sec. 3, Fig. 1). The pooled share is not a typical task (task-level tool-output share P10-P90 23.0-78.1%).
   - *unaffected*: Fresh claim; descriptive accounting, not a policy result.
   - anchor `Sec. 3 "Object types differ in volume and residency"` @ arXiv:2608.31057v1 (HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 eb9a7f58ef69): "Tool outputs account for 55.5% of pooled content volume and 40.2% of retention-weighted cost; artifacts account for 28.3% and 38.9%, respectively (Figure 1)."
2. Calibration gain did not transfer: object-aware policy minus FIFO in repeated tool calls is -1.633 on 15 calibration tasks (Holm p=0.0146) but -0.500 on 8 held-out tasks with only 2 nonzero pairs (Holm p=0.5); no held-out contrast survives Holm correction, and the authors state this is not equivalence (Sec. 4.1, Table 2).
   - *unaffected*: Fresh claim; n=8 held-out, wide CIs; the transfer failure is shown, equivalence is not.
   - anchor `Sec. 4.1 after Table 2` @ arXiv:2608.31057v1 (HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 eb9a7f58ef69): "The held-out split changes the evidentiary status of the calibration result: a policy tuned on development tasks can show a sizable development contrast on the repeated-call metric that its own held-out sweep does not confirm."
3. A shared token cap does not equalise delivered context: none of the ten constrained-arm pairs is matched (larger median <= 1.10x smaller) on all eight complete tasks; FIFO vs adapted retrieval matches on 6 of 8 with a largest gap of 18.7% (Sec. 5).
   - *unaffected*: Fresh claim.
   - anchor `Sec. 5 "Shared caps control admissible state but not delivered context"` @ arXiv:2608.31057v1 (HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 eb9a7f58ef69): "None of the ten constrained-arm pairs matches on all eight complete tasks; FIFO–GA matches on six of eight, with a largest gap of 18.7%."
4. Management work is part of the comparison: over 8 complete tasks adapted retrieval adds 285 importance-rating LLM calls and object-aware compression 169 summary calls; embedding CPU work is uncounted; mean whole-run wall-time retrieval-minus-FIFO is +67.45 s (Sec. 5, Table 3).
   - *unaffected*: Fresh claim.
   - anchor `Sec. 5 "Management work is part of the comparison"` @ arXiv:2608.31057v1 (HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 eb9a7f58ef69): "GA adds 285 importance calls and OA 169 summary calls (Table 3); CPU embedding work is additional and uncounted."
5. The primary endpoint is a process metric (repeated identical tool calls: same tool name and sorted-key JSON args as an earlier call), not repair success; the 48 complete retrieval-study cells lack valid formal repair evaluation, so no claim about task-outcome benefit of any memory policy is supported. Proposed four reporting levels: stored state, delivered context, management work, task/process outcome (Sec. 2, Table 4).
   - *unaffected*: Fresh claim; outcome validity caveat is the authors' own.
   - anchor `Sec. 2 "Populations and outcomes"` @ arXiv:2608.31057v1 (HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 eb9a7f58ef69): "the later 48 complete task–arm cells lack valid formal repair evaluation"

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-22 Stage-2b dive (combined pass): read arXiv HTML v1 in full (main text + Appendix F protocol history; Appendices A-E skimmed by grep for held-out/leakage/protocol). Established that the paper concerns task-local working memory, not cross-session lesson reuse (Stage-1 framing reattributed). Numbers in claims 2-5 checked against Table 2/3 and Sec. 4-5 text.

## intake-2396#record — Agent Memory Distillation: Empowering Small LLM Agents with Hierarchical Teacher Memory

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07169
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance high; categories: memory_augmented, training_distillation, agent_architecture, benchmark_methodology)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2387#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. AMD builds three memory types (workflow, subtask, function) from SUCCESSFUL GPT-5-mini teacher trajectories and injects workflow+subtask at task start and function memory only after a tool error; reported average gains over zero-shot across four 4-8B students are +27.2 pp (AppWorld), +11.2 pp (BFCL V3), +3.4 pp (ToolSandbox) (Sec. 4.2, Table 1). The authors' own scope limit: only text tool-use/function-calling benchmarks, open-ended coding tasks not evaluated, memory frozen offline (Limitations); the coding-agent counterpart by overlapping authors is already indexed (intake-425#record).
   - *narrowed*: Gains are real on the three tool-use benchmarks but measured under same-benchmark memory construction (claim 1); the disjoint re-run covers one student only.
   - anchor `Sec. 4.2 AMD vs. Baselines` @ arXiv:2608.07169v1 (HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 28c39e1ad189): "AMD achieves average accuracy gains of 27.2%p, 11.2%p, and 3.4%p over zero-shot across the four student models on AppWorld, BFCL V3, and ToolSandbox, respectively."
   - anchor `Limitations` @ arXiv:2608.07169v1 (HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 28c39e1ad189): "Such settings include multimodal environments in which agents must ground actions in visual observations, as well as coding tasks, where the action space is not a fixed set of callable operations but open-ended code that the agent must generate from scratch."
1. The headline protocol is same-benchmark: the teacher runs the evaluated task set, memory is distilled from it, and the student retrieves from it on that set (Sec. 3.1; App. B.3 states main experiments distill and apply memory within the same benchmark). The headline numbers therefore mix real transfer with same-task leakage.
   - *unaffected*: Stated by the authors.
   - anchor `App. B.3 first paragraph` @ arXiv:2608.07169v1 (HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 28c39e1ad189): "In our main experiments, memory is distilled and applied within the same benchmark."
2. Disjoint check (App. B.3, Table 6; Qwen3-4B only, one run per cell, no intervals; baselines NOT re-run): cross-split 7:3 and self-excluded retrieval keep large gains. AppWorld 14.88->46.43 (self-excluded) and 16.07->41.07 (cross-split) vs 49.40 in the main table; BFCL 15.50->31.00 and 16.13->30.65 vs 38.50; ToolSandbox 16.28->23.26 and 16.67->22.92 vs 20.16. Retained gain fraction relative to the main-table gain: AppWorld ~91% (self-excluded) / ~72% (cross-split); BFCL ~67% / ~63%; ToolSandbox exceeds the main-table gain. The authors' wording that the disjoint results "stay close" understates the BFCL drop (~7.5 pp).
   - *narrowed*: Authors' "stay close" holds for AppWorld self-excluded (-3 pp) but BFCL loses ~7.5 pp (38.50 -> 31.00/30.65); sample sizes per cell are small (derived eval denominators 56/62/48 for cross-split) and no intervals; one student; baselines not run under the disjoint protocol, so "outperforms all baselines" is untested under disjointness.
   - anchor `App. B.3 Results` @ arXiv:2608.07169v1 (HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 28c39e1ad189): "Since no task can rely on memory from itself in either setup, these results indicate that the improvements come from the utility of the distilled memory rather than from memory construction and evaluation sharing the same tasks."
   - anchor `App. B.3 Self-excluded retrieval` @ arXiv:2608.07169v1 (HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 28c39e1ad189): "We distill memory over the full benchmark, but during evaluation, each task retrieves memory distilled from other tasks, never its own."
3. DIVE-ADDED (not in the paper): self-excluded retrieval excludes only a task's own memory. AppWorld tasks are generated three per scenario by varying placeholder values (AppWorld paper: 250 scenarios x 3 tasks; Test-N 168 tasks = 56 scenarios), so sibling tasks from the same scenario stay in the memory pool; this residual same-scenario leakage is not excluded by the protocol. Whether the cross-split respects scenarios is not stated (the derived AppWorld eval size 9/56 = 16.07% equals one third of 168, not the stated 30%; unexplained).
   - *uncertain*: Dive-added. Same-scenario sibling leakage in self-excluded retrieval is inferred from the AppWorld paper's task structure plus the paper's task-level exclusion wording; the AMD authors do not state whether scenario siblings are excluded. Not tested.
   - anchor `AppWorld paper Sec. 4 Final Dataset` @ arXiv:2407.18901 (HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 6814afaee1cc): "Our benchmark has 250 task scenarios with 3 tasks each, totaling 750 tasks, split into Train (105), Dev (60), Test-N (168), and Test-C (417) splits."
   - anchor `AppWorld paper Sec. 3 task generation` @ arXiv:2407.18901 (HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 6814afaee1cc): "We instantiate multiple tasks per scenario by carefully varying the placeholder values (apparel, last-color, and preferred-color) and starting states for each task."
4. Self-generated memory is near zero-shot on AppWorld: Qwen3-4B student-memory 16.07 vs zero-shot 14.88 vs AMD 49.40 (Table 2; on BFCL the student-memory arm is 27.00 vs 15.50 zero-shot vs 38.50 AMD). Baselines ReasoningBank/MemP/SASM sometimes fall below zero-shot (e.g. ReasoningBank Qwen3-4B AppWorld 10.71 vs 14.88).
   - *unaffected*: Fresh claim.
   - anchor `Sec. 5.1 ablation text` @ arXiv:2608.07169v1 (HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 28c39e1ad189): "We also compare against a variant that uses student-generated memories in place of teacher memories (Student Memory), which yields results close to zero-shot performance on AppWorld and substantially below AMD on both benchmarks."
5. More injected memory hurts for small students: subtask memory accuracy falls monotonically from 49.40 (k=1) to 33.34 (k=5) on AppWorld/Qwen3-4B; the interference explanation is the authors' unproven attribution (Sec. 5.4). Teacher accuracy is not monotone in student gain for the 4B student (Table 3: GPT-5-mini teacher 50.00% beats DeepSeek V4 Pro 81.55% for Qwen3-4B).
   - *unaffected*: Fresh claim; mechanism untested.
   - anchor `Sec. 5.4` @ arXiv:2608.07169v1 (HTML, retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 28c39e1ad189): "where accuracy drops monotonically from 49.40% to 33.34% as"

The headline +27.2/+11.2/+3.4 pp gains use same-benchmark memory; the paper's own disjoint re-run (App. B.3) retains most but not all of the gain (BFCL ~two-thirds), for one student, single run, with no baseline comparison. ## intake-2397#record — SWE-chat: Coding Agent Interactions From Real Users in the Wild

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.20779
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: benchmark_methodology, agent_architecture, knowledge_management)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2387#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Corpus: SWE-chat v2 holds almost 18,000 real coding-agent sessions, more than 229,000 user prompts and 2 million tool calls from 700+ public GitHub repos, collected via developers opting into Entire.io checkpoint logging; about 69% of sessions are Claude Code, the rest Codex, OpenCode, Gemini CLI, Cursor and others (Sec. 2). It is a living dataset; the paper reports the September 2026 release.
   - *unaffected*: Fresh claim.
   - anchor `Abstract` @ arXiv:2604.20779v2 (HTML, v2 of 1 Oct 2026, retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 830aca18f570): "The dataset currently contains almost 18,000 sessions, comprising more than 229,000 user prompts and 2 million agent tool calls."
1. In Claude Code sessions users push back after about 46% of prompts and interrupt in 3.5-5.2% of turns, 50.2% combined (Sec. 4.4, Fig. 8). The abstract's "50% of all turns" is Claude-Code-only data (about 69% of the corpus), per the Sec. 4.4 comparability note.
   - *narrowed*: Abstract says 50% of all turns; Fig. 8 is Claude Code only.
   - anchor `Sec. 4.4 first paragraph` @ arXiv:2604.20779v2 (HTML, v2 of 1 Oct 2026, retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 830aca18f570): "For comparability with McCain et al. [28], we only include data from Claude Code for the results in Figure 8."
2. The pushback labels are produced by a 9B open-weight LLM judge (Qwen3.5-9B) given the preceding transcript: accuracy against 100 human-gold labels is 0.67 (4-class) and 0.79 (binary pushback); human-human kappa on 90 items is 0.832 (4-class). The authors explicitly decline to draw conclusive statements from LLM-generated labels (App. A, App. F.1).
   - *unaffected*: Judge accuracy figures from Table 8 row qwen-3.5-9b (0.67 / 0.79); kappa from Table 6.
   - anchor `App. A Limitations` @ arXiv:2604.20779v2 (HTML, v2 of 1 Oct 2026, retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 830aca18f570): "For results based on LLM-generated labels, we do not draw conclusive statements, given the inherent unreliability of such annotations and the risk of LLM hacking [4]."
3. The "correction" class is defined broadly: it includes the user "changing requirements/direction/scope mid-task", not only correcting an agent error (App. F.2.4 prompt). A pushback rate is therefore an upper bound on the agent-error correction rate, not an agent-error rate.
   - *unaffected*: Fresh claim.
   - anchor `App. F.2.4 pushback classifier prompt` @ arXiv:2604.20779v2 (HTML, v2 of 1 Oct 2026, retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 830aca18f570): "correction - The user redirects the agent by providing missing context, correcting a misunderstanding, pointing out factual errors, or changing requirements/direction/scope mid-task."
4. Capture selection: sessions whose output the user abandons entirely are not committed and thus not captured, which the authors say likely overestimates session success (App. A). The source does not measure whether a corrected behaviour recurs across sessions or whether any instruction/memory file changed agent behaviour (searched the v2 text for across-session / cross-session / same-mistake / learn-from: no relevant hit); that part of the Stage-1 question is NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE.
   - *unaffected*: Absence claim covers the v2 HTML text only; the Entire.io and SWE-chat repos were not fetched.
   - anchor `App. A Limitations` @ arXiv:2604.20779v2 (HTML, v2 of 1 Oct 2026, retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 830aca18f570): "If the user abandons the agent’s output entirely, session logs are not committed and thus not captured by our data."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-22 Stage-2b dive (combined pass): read v2 HTML (abstract, Sec. 1-2, Sec. 4.4, App. A, App. F annotation validation and the pushback classifier prompt in full; Sec. 3, 4.1-4.3 and App. B-E by search only). The source measures in-session human pushback, not cross-session recurrence or instruction-file effects; pushback rate includes scope changes and is LLM-labelled. | 2026-10-22 Stage-2 close-out (operator pre-approved all recommended sources): dive-surfaced sources NOT dived, with reasons: SWE-INTERACT 2606.30573, SecureForge 2605.08382 and SLEIGHT-bench 2605.16626 declined (not lesson-relevant).

## intake-2398#record — Continuum: Efficient and Robust Multi-Turn LLM Agent Scheduling with KV Cache Time-to-Live

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.02230
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance high; categories: kv_cache, inference_serving, agent_architecture, context_management)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2389#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Abstract + Intro: end-of-turn eviction (finished requests lose KV when others wait) breaks agent loops; Continuum pins the KV of requests that emit a tool call for a per-request time-to-live (TTL) set from reload/prefill cost plus the per-turn queueing delay an eviction would cause, and auto-unpins on expiry.
   - *unaffected*: Read in abstract and §1/§4; mechanism as stated.
   - anchor `Abstract, extracted text L103 @ v7` @ arXiv:2511.02230v7 (08 Sep 2026), HTML retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 c3ac6d3b1827: "Continnum selectively pins the KV cache in GPU memory with a time-to-live value determined by the reload cost and potential queueing delay induced by eviction."
1. §4.2 / §5.1: TTL = argmax of expected net benefit over the empirical CDF of per-tool durations; durations are measured server-side as the interval between a request finishing with a tool call and the next request with the same program_id arriving; candidates are the recorded durations; cold-start falls back from per-tool records to global records to a fixed TTL derived from an exponential-duration, fully-memoryful assumption.
   - *unaffected*: Algorithm 1 and §4.2 math were stripped in the HTML text extraction (symbols lost); the prose description and §5.1 handler were read in full. The argmax formula itself is not quoted.
   - anchor `§5.1, extracted text L478-L479 @ v7` @ arXiv:2511.02230v7 (08 Sep 2026), HTML retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 c3ac6d3b1827: "tracks per-tool latency using observed"
2. §4.1: the benefit has a second term besides reload cost - the queueing delay a returning program pays behind other requests after eviction - so retention can be justified even when reload is cheap; this term vanishes for memoryless (geometric-length) programs and is largest when programs have a fixed number of turns (a shortest-job-first approximation).
   - *unaffected*: Key differentiator vs InferCept; stated by the authors, supported only by their own re-implementation of InferCept (Fig 4).
   - anchor `§4.1, extracted text L426 @ v7` @ arXiv:2511.02230v7 (08 Sep 2026), HTML retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 c3ac6d3b1827: "in this case, pinning provides no benefit for the queueing delay since keeping the order does not accelerate finishing short jobs first. In contrast, if each program issues a fixed number of requests, then the TTL can eliminate the queueing cost by approximating Shortest Job First."
3. §5.2: deadlock prevention - when a request cannot be scheduled because pins hold the pool, pins are released starting from the program that arrived latest; TTL-aware priority orders preempted requests first, then pinned (within-TTL) requests, then program-level arrival order.
   - *unaffected*: Prose in §5.2 and §4.3 read.
   - anchor `§5.2, extracted text L519 @ v7` @ arXiv:2511.02230v7 (08 Sep 2026), HTML retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 c3ac6d3b1827: "we iteratively selects victims from pinned_requests with the latest program arrival time to unpin and free the space until the first request can be scheduled to run."
4. §6: evaluated by trace replay of mini-swe-agent/SWE-Bench, BFCL-v4 web-search and OpenHands traces (GPT-5-generated) on vLLM 0.10.2 (+LMCache 0.3.7 for the offload arms) with Llama-3.1-8B/70B, Gemma-3-12B on A100/H100/B200: 1.12-3.66x lower delay and 1.10-3.22x higher throughput; up to 8.18x only on an anonymized internal H100 testbed with real SWE-agents; the abstract headline "over 8x" is that testbed figure, not the three-setup range. Per-figure values and run-to-run variance are NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE (figure-only).
   - *narrowed*: Abstract says "over 8x" average JCT; the body supports 8.18x only for the internal testbed. No variance/repetition information is in the text; figure values not extracted.
   - anchor `§1, extracted text L166 @ v7` @ arXiv:2511.02230v7 (08 Sep 2026), HTML retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 c3ac6d3b1827: "Across three hardware and model setups, we show that Continnum reduces delay by 1.12x to 3.66x and improves throughput by 1.10x to 3.22x on multi-turn agentic workloads."
5. Scope (§3.1, §7): designed for ReAct-style tool gaps (SWE-Bench tool time mean 925 ms, BFCL mean 1.9 s, heavy-tailed: slowest 10% of `cd` calls carry 94.1% of the delay); human-paced gaps, speculative branches and context folding are not modelled. The InferCept comparison is the authors own re-implementation on vLLM+LMCache with a modified cost estimate, not the original system.
   - *narrowed*: Scope limit relevant to EPYC: no human-paced gaps, no hybrid/recurrent state, no checkpoint-quantised resume.
   - anchor `§7, extracted text L694 @ v7` @ arXiv:2511.02230v7 (08 Sep 2026), HTML retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 c3ac6d3b1827: "The current design of Continnum are optimized for ReAct-style, tool-interleaving agents where each LLM step returns a clear tool invocation followed by a gap before the next step."
   - anchor `Fig 5 caption, extracted text L272 @ v7` @ arXiv:2511.02230v7 (08 Sep 2026), HTML retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 c3ac6d3b1827: "Functions’ execution time can be extremely long-tailed. Slowest 10% of fetch_url account for 52.5% of the total delay, while slowest 10% of cd account for 94.1%."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-22 Stage-2b dive (combined pass): entry created from a FULL read of arXiv v7 (the index only had Continuum as a reference id inside intake-1816#record/intake-1785#record notes). Headline narrowed (8x is an internal-testbed figure); InferCept critique flagged as authors re-implementation; human-paced gaps confirmed out of scope.

## intake-2399#record — Cost-Efficient Large Language Model Serving for Multi-turn Conversations with CachedAttention

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.19708
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty low, relevance medium; categories: kv_cache, inference_serving, context_management)
- **Disposition**: knowledge_only
- **Expanded from**: intake-2389#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Abstract/§4.2: saving per-session KV in a host-DRAM + SSD store (AttentionStore) instead of discarding it between turns cuts TTFT by up to 87%, raises prefill throughput up to 7.8x and end-to-end cost up to 70% (LLaMA-13B/65B/70B, Falcon-40B on 4xA100, 128 GB DRAM + 10 TB SSD, PCIe Gen4 ~26 GB/s).
   - *unaffected*: Numbers are author-reported on their own testbed; page cross-references Fig 16 for Figs 14-17 (typo in source).
   - anchor `Abstract/Intro, extracted text L72 @ v3` @ arXiv:2403.19708v3 (30 Jun 2024), HTML retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 295189fddc21: "CachedAttention significantly decreases the time to the first token (TTFT) by up to 87%, improves the prompt prefilling throughput by up to 7.8 for multi-turn conversations, and reduces the end-to-end inference cost by up to 70%."
1. §3.3: scheduler-aware fetch and eviction - look-ahead windows over the waiting job queue prefetch SSD->DRAM for queued jobs and evict the entry whose job sits furthest back; this beats LRU/FIFO (hit rate 86% vs 58%/48% at 128G/10T; >99.6% of hits served from DRAM).
   - *unaffected*: Look-ahead window sizes tied to storage capacity; read in §3.3.
   - anchor `§4.3.3, extracted text L259 @ v3` @ arXiv:2403.19708v3 (30 Jun 2024), HTML retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 295189fddc21: "With the increased SSD capacity (128G/10T), CA achieves a remarkable hit rate of 86%, surpassing LRU (58%) and FIFO (48%). CA achieves high overall hit rates because CA is aware of the future KV cache access information to avoid evicting the KV caches that will be used in the future. The higher h..."
2. §4.3.6: capacity rule - with a per-session TTL of one hour, provisioning 0.25 of the worst-case capacity (distinct-sessions-per-unit-time x max-context KV) already gives a 98% hit rate; 0.1 gives 51%.
   - *unaffected*: The TTL here is the retention bound of the store, not tuned per tool; it is what TraceLab sweeps (1 min -> 1 h).
   - anchor `§4.3.6, extracted text L345 @ v3` @ arXiv:2403.19708v3 (30 Jun 2024), HTML retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 295189fddc21: "In the experiment, we set the TTL to one hour. As shown in Figure 23(a), when the ratio is 0.1, we achieve the cache hit rate of 51%. When the ratio is 0.25, we achieve the cache hit rate of 98%."
3. §3.2: layer-wise pre-loading and asynchronous write-back hide the DRAM<->HBM transfer behind compute (pre-loading with a sufficient buffer cut prefill time 61%; async saving 13-15%).
   - *unaffected*: Fig-level overlap numbers read from prose in §4.3.2 only.
4. Workload caveat (§4.1): request/session arrival times are synthetic Poisson over ShareGPT, which has no timestamps - so no real human think-time or tool-gap distribution is evaluated; only capacity-vs-hit-rate is.
   - *narrowed*: No real inter-turn gap data; this is the main reason the paper does not answer the human-paced-gap question.
   - anchor `§4.1, extracted text L218 @ v3` @ arXiv:2403.19708v3 (30 Jun 2024), HTML retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 295189fddc21: "As there is no public request arrival timestamp available in the dataset, we generate request arrival times based on the Poisson distribution"
5. §3.4: decoupled positional encoding lets truncated sessions keep their saved KV by storing keys before RoPE and re-embedding on load (requires relative PE and an attention-only KV; accuracy parity with recompute on PPL/MMLU/LongEval).
   - *narrowed*: Needs relative PE and pure-attention KV; EPYC production models are hybrid (Gated-DeltaNet recurrent state cannot be truncated), so portable only to the attention layers.
   - anchor `§3.4, extracted text L203 @ v3` @ arXiv:2403.19708v3 (30 Jun 2024), HTML retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 295189fddc21: "By simply moving the time of caching KVs before embedding positional encodings in RPE as shown in Figure 11, CachedAttention can store the KVs without embedded positional encodings in AttentionStore. When reusing the KVs in CachedAttention, the KVs are embedded with the new positional encodings a..."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-22 Stage-2b dive (combined pass): entry created; previously only a referenced id inside intake-1324#record. Read §1-§4.3.8 and §5; appendix/refs not read. Found that the only gap-related parameter (1 h TTL) is a retention bound, with synthetic arrivals - it does not evidence any idle-gap policy.

## intake-2400#record — InferCept: Efficient Intercept Support for Augmented Large Language Model Inference

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.01869
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance high; categories: kv_cache, inference_serving, agent_architecture, tool_implementation)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2389#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Abstract/§3.2: treating a tool call or human reply as end-of-request (Discard) forces recomputation of the context, 37-40% of total forward time on a mixed workload; Preserve (keep KV on GPU) and Swap (to CPU) each waste a different resource, so no single policy wins across interception types.
   - *unaffected*: Source text has x-signs stripped in extraction (1.6-2 means 1.6-2x).
   - anchor `Abstract, extracted text L50-L57 @ v2` @ arXiv:2402.01869v2 (30 May 2024), HTML retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 67ead9958534: "which accounts for 37-40% of total model forwarding time."
1. §4.3: per intercepted request, choose min(WastePreserve, WasteChunkDiscard) where waste = GPU-memory x time; the swap budget (bytes the GPU<->CPU link can move hidden behind a forward pass) is then spent on the highest-waste requests first; resumed requests are scheduled by original arrival time.
   - *unaffected*: Equations 1-5 symbols stripped by extraction; prose read in full.
   - anchor `§4.3, extracted text L247 @ v2` @ arXiv:2402.01869v2 (30 May 2024), HTML retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 67ead9958534: "we calculate its memory waste as the minimum of WastePreserve (Equation 2) and WasteChunkDiscard (Equation 4):"
2. §4.4: the preserve cost needs the interception duration; without an oracle InferCept uses the time elapsed since the interception began as the estimate (longer pause => larger estimated remaining), reaching 93% of oracle performance on the mixed workload.
   - *unaffected*: Elapsed-time estimator is equivalent to LRU victim choice under heavy-tailed pauses; this equivalence is our inference, not the paper statement.
   - anchor `§4.4, extracted text L264 @ v2` @ arXiv:2402.01869v2 (30 May 2024), HTML retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 67ead9958534: "Our evaluation shows that InferCept using this estimation method achieves 93% of the performance compared with using an oracle providing exact interception durations in mixed workload."
3. §2.2/§5.1: interception types span ~1e-4 s (calculator) to ~29 s (chatbot human reply); the human reply time is NOT measured but modelled as reading time plus typing time; results: 1.6-2x higher sustainable request rate and 2x more completed requests/s vs vLLM Discard (A100, GPT-J-6B / Vicuna-13B / Llama3-70B, vLLM base).
   - *narrowed*: Human gap is a model (reading + typing), so the chatbot arm cannot speak to real idle gaps; TraceLab has the measured distribution.
   - anchor `§2.2, extracted text L156 @ v2` @ arXiv:2402.01869v2 (30 May 2024), HTML retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 67ead9958534: "We estimate human response time (i.e., the interception time) as the summation of human scanning time (Brysbaert, 2019) of the previous chat response and typing time (Ma et al., 2015) of the next prompt."
   - anchor `Abstract, extracted text L57 @ v2` @ arXiv:2402.01869v2 (30 May 2024), HTML retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 67ead9958534: "InferCept improves the overall serving throughput by 1.6-2 and completes 2 more requests per second compared to the state-of-the-art LLM inference systems."
4. §5.2: stepwise ablation on the 6B mixed workload at 2 req/s (each step relative to the bar before it): original-arrival-time ordering -24.5% normalised latency, recompute chunking -7.8%, budgeted swap -12.7%, adding Preserve with a crude pause-type rule (Discard for interactive/long pauses, Preserve for short automated ones) -46.1%, the full adaptive min-waste policy a further -46.4%; i.e. a pause-type hint alone captures a step of the same order as the adaptive policy.
   - *unaffected*: Percentages are per-step reductions vs the preceding bar (Fig 3 prose), not shares of a total; 6B mixed workload at 2 req/s only.
   - anchor `§5.2, extracted text L301 @ v2` @ arXiv:2402.01869v2 (30 May 2024), HTML retrieved 2026-10-22, sha256-12 67ead9958534: "discard for interactive (or long-running) interceptions and preserve for automated (short-running) interceptions. This addition has a 46.1% improvement, mainly because of the added support for Preserve. Finally, adding min-waste-based adaptive schedule (i.e., the whole InferCept) results in"

## intake-2401#record — llguidance: Low-level Guidance (constrained decoding / structured outputs engine)

- **URL**: https://github.com/guidance-ai/llguidance
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty medium, relevance high; categories: tool_implementation, inference_serving, speculative_decoding)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2388#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Mask cost (README L78, L96-101 @ 6cef6ed): ~50 us single-core CPU per mask for a 128k tokenizer on JSONSchemaBench (2.5M tokens, 10k schemas); an un-sliced full mask for a typical JSON schema is ~1.5 ms; <1% of masks exceed 1 ms, 0.001% exceed 10 ms, max < 30 ms. Vendor-measured on their bench, not independently reproduced here; llama.cpp docs/llguidance.md L28 restates it (p99 0.5 ms, p100 20 ms).
   - *unaffected*: fresh claim; vendor number, flagged as restatement-only (no independent reproduction read)
   - anchor `README.md:L96-L101 @ 6cef6ed` @ guidance-ai/llguidance@6cef6ed9c973c2a155a85f2bb1996320af28b289 (2026-10-14): "In llguidance, the full mask computation for a typical JSON schema takes about 1.5ms (for 128k tokenizer). However, very often the ["slicer" optimization](./docs/optimizations.md#slicer-optimization) applies, and thus the avarage mask computation in [JSON Schema Bench](https://github.com/guidance..."
1. Slicer optimization (docs/optimizations.md L109-198): token-trie partitions under-approximating unconstrained contexts (e.g. JSON string bodies) let a whole slice be OR-ed into the mask without walking it; about 10x faster on MaskBench; 53.8% of masks sliced at 61 us, 0.5% un-sliced 85%+ masks at 1577 us. Default slices are used when LlgTokenizerInit.slices is NULL (v1.0.1 header L230-235), which is what llama.cpp passes (common/llguidance.cpp L196).
   - *unaffected*: fresh claim
   - anchor `docs/optimizations.md:L177 @ 6cef6ed` @ guidance-ai/llguidance@6cef6ed9c973c2a155a85f2bb1996320af28b289: "This optimization make the mask computation about 10x faster in [MaskBench](https://github.com/guidance-ai/jsonschemabench/tree/main/maskbench)."
2. Fast-forward (forced) tokens (docs/fast_forward.md): forced bytes are tokenized then up to ~4 trailing tokens are dropped when a longer token could span the forced boundary (canonical-tokenization guard); with that guard MaskBench shows no non-canonical forced tokens, without it 97% are non-canonical; forced tokens are 12.1-12.7% of MaskBench tokens.
   - *unaffected*: fresh claim; MaskBench whitespace config for the 12.1-12.7% figure NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE
   - anchor `docs/fast_forward.md:L129-L132 @ 6cef6ed` @ guidance-ai/llguidance@6cef6ed9c973c2a155a85f2bb1996320af28b289: "Note, that we can conservatively skip `grammar_allows()` check in the algorithm above, and thus just compute once and for all the set of tokens that are not allowed as the last token in forced bytes. This drops the proportion of forced tokens in maskbench from `12.7%` to `12.1%`."
3. The C API (parser/llguidance.h v1.0.1 L487-578) exposes compute_mask, consume_token(s), rollback, is_accepting, validate_tokens and compute_ff_tokens. llama.cpp production-consolidated-v10 (ffc1bac8, common/llguidance.cpp L1-258) calls ONLY new_matcher, consume_token, get_mask/compute_mask, reset, clone: no compute_ff_tokens, no validate_tokens, no rollback. Its apply() computes the FULL vocab mask even for the 1-token grammar check at common/sampling.cpp L605-615, so the sample-then-check shortcut does not save mask work under llguidance.
   - *narrowed*: narrows the intake-2388#record prior-dive premise "LLGuidance is an alternative": turning it ON does not add jump-forward and makes the common no-rejection step do a full mask walk instead of a 1-token check
   - anchor `common/llguidance.cpp:L49-L59 @ ffc1bac8 (llama.cpp production-consolidated-v10)` @ llama.cpp ffc1bac82eeca6f9099e1ccd9ba49703c460a115: "const uint32_t * mask = llg_matcher_get_mask(ctx->grammar); if (mask == nullptr) { if (llg_matcher_compute_mask(ctx->grammar) == 0) { mask = llg_matcher_get_mask(ctx->grammar); } else { LOG_ERR("llg error: %s\n", llg_matcher_get_error(ctx->grammar)); llg_free_matcher(ctx->grammar); ctx->grammar =..."
4. JSON schema whitespace (docs/json_schema.md L70-86): whitespace is flexible by default (same forced-chain break as GBNF SPACE_RULE); a top-level "x-guidance": {"whitespace_flexible": false} (or item/key_separator, whitespace_pattern) gives compact JSON. llama.cpp passes the schema verbatim ("%llguidance {}\nstart: %json " + schema.dump(), json-schema-to-grammar.cpp L1159-1163), so this is a per-request opt-in with no kernel change.
   - *narrowed*: overturns any assumption that LLGUIDANCE=ON fixes the SPACE_RULE forced-chain break by itself; it does not unless the request carries x-guidance whitespace_flexible=false
   - anchor `docs/json_schema.md:L81 @ 6cef6ed` @ guidance-ai/llguidance@6cef6ed9c973c2a155a85f2bb1996320af28b289: "- `whitespace_flexible`, defaults to `true`; set to `false` to enforce compact JSON representation"
5. Build and opt-in in llama.cpp (common/CMakeLists.txt L138-164; sampling.cpp L207-212): LLAMA_LLGUIDANCE=ON pulls llguidance via ExternalProject at pinned v1.0.1 (d795912, 2025-07-03) and runs cargo build (Rust >= 1.87 per README at HEAD), so the build needs network access and a Rust toolchain. Requests opt in with a grammar string starting "%llguidance"; when ON, EVERY json_schema request routes to llguidance (no caller passes force_gbnf=true). When OFF (production), a "%llguidance" grammar hits GGML_ABORT inside common_sampler_init, which the server try/catch (server-context.cpp L1830-1836) cannot catch.
   - *unaffected*: fresh claim; GGML_ABORT reachability is a source read, not exercised
   - anchor `common/sampling.cpp:L207-L212 @ ffc1bac8` @ llama.cpp ffc1bac82eeca6f9099e1ccd9ba49703c460a115: "if (grammar_str.compare(0, 11, "%llguidance") == 0) { #ifdef LLAMA_USE_LLGUIDANCE grmr = llama_sampler_init_llg(vocab, "lark", grammar_str.c_str()); #else GGML_ABORT("llguidance (cmake -DLLAMA_LLGUIDANCE=ON) is not enabled"); #endif // LLAMA_USE_LLGUIDANCE"

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-22 Stage-2b dive (combined pass): read llguidance README/docs at 6cef6ed and the v1.0.1 C header pinned by llama.cpp, plus the full llama.cpp v10 integration. Established that llama.cpp uses none of ff_tokens/rollback/validate_tokens, computes a full mask on every grammar-checked step, keeps flexible JSON whitespace by default, silently routes all json_schema requests to llguidance when ON (with GBNF-incompatible semantics: additionalProperties default true), is fail-open on matcher error, and that the OFF production build aborts the process on a "%llguidance" grammar. | 2026-10-22 Stage-2 close-out (operator pre-approved all recommended sources): dive-surfaced sources NOT dived, with reasons: dottxt coalescence post and guidance commit 5ad05eb8 declined (restatements of fast_forward.md); arXiv 2309.08715 was ingested as intake-2423#record.

## intake-2402#record — Fast JSON Decoding for Local LLMs with Compressed Finite State Machine

- **URL**: https://www.lmsys.org/blog/2024-02-05-compressed-fsm/
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty low, relevance medium; categories: tool_implementation, inference_serving, speculative_decoding)
- **Disposition**: knowledge_only
- **Expanded from**: intake-2388#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Jump-forward (blog L60-74): from the regex FSM, consecutive singular transition edges are compressed into singular paths; instead of decoding those token by token, the forced span is prefilled (extended) in one step up to the next branching point.
   - *unaffected*: fresh claim
   - anchor `blog/2024-02-05-compressed-fsm.md:L74 @ lm-sys/lm-sys.github.io 20c9f9d41007` @ lm-sys/lm-sys.github.io main 70947b1c, file last changed 20c9f9d41007 (2024-07-24); retrieved 2026-10-22: "That is precisely how the jump-forward decoding algorithm makes decoding faster. In the jump-forward algorithm, we examine the finite state machine of the given regular expression, identify all the singular transition edges, and compress consecutive ones together into **singular paths**. Instead ..."
1. Implementation (L79-80): in SGLang a jump-forward terminates the request and enqueues a new one; RadixAttention prefix reuse keeps the KV of earlier tokens, so only the forced span is prefilled.
   - *unaffected*: fresh claim
   - anchor `blog/2024-02-05-compressed-fsm.md:L80` @ lm-sys/lm-sys.github.io 20c9f9d41007: "When executing a jump-forward, we can simply terminate the current request and enqueue a new one. The RadixAttention and efficient **extend** primitive in the SGLang runtime will automatically reuse the KV cache of the previous tokens, thereby avoiding redundant computation."
2. Retokenization caveat (L82-113): different tokenizations of the forced span change the next-token distribution and can cause pathologies (e.g. endless decoding when the model wants a merged token like `",` that the regex forbids); SGLang appends the forced STRING and re-tokenizes the whole text, at ~4% overhead; a single whole-output regex is preferred over concatenated regexes.
   - *unaffected*: fresh claim
   - anchor `blog/2024-02-05-compressed-fsm.md:L112` @ lm-sys/lm-sys.github.io 20c9f9d41007: "- We have implemented a re-tokenization mechanism during the jump-forward phase. This involves appending the string instead of the tokens, followed by a re-tokenization of the entire text. This method effectively resolves most tokenization issues and results in only a minor increase in computatio..."
3. Results (L10, L117-133): llama-7B on one A10 vs vLLM 0.2.7 / guidance 0.1.0 / outlines 0.2.5 / llama.cpp v0.2.38 Python binding; up to 2x lower latency (batch 1) and up to 2.5x throughput (max batch). Numbers are figure-only (result.png not read as data), vendor-run, 2024 baselines.
   - *narrowed*: speedups are 2024 GPU baselines, figure-only; llama.cpp baseline was the Python binding v0.2.38, no bearing on v10 numbers
   - anchor `blog/2024-02-05-compressed-fsm.md:L10` @ lm-sys/lm-sys.github.io 20c9f9d41007: "Distinct from existing systems that decode one token at one step, our method analyzes the finite state machine of a regular expression, compresses singular transition paths, and decodes <u>multiple tokens in a single step</u> whenever feasible. In comparison to state-of-the-art systems (guidance ..."
4. Dive-added (SGLang HEAD d0a7565, 2026-10-22): the jump-forward interface survives in python/sglang/srt/constrained (try_jump_forward / jump_forward_str_state / jump_and_retokenize in the outlines, xgrammar, llguidance and reasoner backends; the llguidance backend sources it from compute_ff_tokens), but git grep of python/sglang/srt finds NO caller of these methods, and the outlines backend hard-sets jump_forward_map = None. Jump-forward is dormant in the current SGLang runtime.
   - *narrowed*: the blog is a design reference only; the shipping reference implementation is no longer active in SGLang srt
   - anchor `python/sglang/srt/constrained/outlines_backend.py:L160-L161 @ d0a7565` @ sgl-project/sglang@d0a75654368a470d8484e3ea45b06e500fee57af (2026-10-22): "jump_forward_map = None return OutlinesGrammar(guide, jump_forward_map)"

### Dive corrections

Companion to the SGLang paper intake-041#record (jump-forward is also described there); distinct artifact.

The bench_serving dataset is `random` only (`choices=['random']`), closed-loop via `--max-concurrency` (semaphore) or gamma-spaced `--request-rate`: no prefix reuse, no agent shape, no mixed long-prompt arrivals unless the operator builds them; backends include openai (completions) and openai-chat, `--ignore-eos` is supported, and the client is single-process and documented as client-bound at ultra-high QPS on small models.

   - anchor `benchmark_serving.py --dataset-name @ 2633e9a4` @ SemiAnalysisAI/InferenceX@2633e9a443958ad87d3dbea1cb98bc829712fcaa: "choices=["random"],"
   - anchor `benchmark_serving.py max_concurrency @ 2633e9a4` @ SemiAnalysisAI/InferenceX@2633e9a443958ad87d3dbea1cb98bc829712fcaa: "semaphore = asyncio.Semaphore(max_concurrency) if max_concurrency else None"
   - anchor `inferencex-e2e/infx/bench_serving/KNOWN_LIMITATION.md @ 2633e9a4` @ SemiAnalysisAI/InferenceX@2633e9a443958ad87d3dbea1cb98bc829712fcaa: "the current InferenceX bench_serving client becomes client-bound."
3. InferenceX's agentic benchmark is not bench_serving: it is the AgentX harness (PyPI agentx-harness==1.0.12), a preset over NVIDIA AIPerf replaying the SemiAnalysis cc-traces-weka dataset (default `semianalysis_cc_traces_weka_062126`) against an OpenAI-compatible server; so the 'InferenceX agentic client' and the AIPerf path are one engine.
   - *unaffected*: fresh claim from the pass; read from source on disk
   - anchor `inferencex-e2e/docs/agentx-standalone.md @ 2633e9a4` @ SemiAnalysisAI/InferenceX@2633e9a443958ad87d3dbea1cb98bc829712fcaa: "Run the AgentX client against an already-running OpenAI-compatible server."
   - anchor `aiperf docs/tutorials/weka-trace.md l.7 @ 30420ce5` @ ai-dynamo/aiperf@30420ce581d4fc9eeb34b4d47defe367ab8c3a11 (v0.13.0): "That benchmark is built on this corpus with extra rules locked in."
4. Locals' own 8k-prompt result (p99.9 ITL 12.0 s waiting vs 3.1 s mixed passes at 8 requests) was a synthetic fixed-length run with 3xN prompts rather than InferenceX's 10xN, and Locals itself states that its 2-request run had too few long-prompt arrivals for the 12 s gap to reach p99.9: the figure is only meaningful with a stated arrival count.
   - *unaffected*: fresh claim from the pass; read from source on disk
   - anchor `Locals docs/measurements/README.md:111 @ 0d39e16194e2` @ Generated-Computers/Locals@0d39e16194e2c123ce10695e45619111eb0e45b0: "run had too few long-prompt arrivals for a 12 s gap to reach the p99.9 of its"
   - anchor `Locals docs/measurements/README.md:73 @ 0d39e16194e2` @ Generated-Computers/Locals@0d39e16194e2c123ce10695e45619111eb0e45b0: "InferenceX sends 10 x N prompts per point; we sent"

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-22 Stage-2b dive (combined pass): the module path is inferencex-e2e/infx/bench_serving (not a top-level bench_serving). It is a vLLM benchmark_serving descendant limited to the `random` dataset; InferenceX's agentic numbers come from AgentX/AIPerf, a different engine. The p99.9 ITL is a stock default, but pooled across requests.
